ParlaMint-NO_2011-03-15.ana.ud2502e195	It is absolutely crucial that we ensure that a child who has suffered a care failure, a safe and effective child protection, and that children meet up with adult people in child protection that they can trust and that they feel confident in. We must have a clear law that guarantees children the best protection against abuse or other harmful influence. The changes currently proposed in the Children's Protection Act, on police certification requirements, etc., are very important in order to further strengthen the protection of children in the field of child protection. Safe adults are important in a child’s life. Many of the children who are under the care of child protection have experienced a great lack of this, and many have great difficulties in trusting and trusting in adults. Children shall not experience abuse and care failure or any other harmful influence under the care of child protection. There are many different measures and instruments that need to work together to make children in the care of their children feel safe and to form good, safe relationships with adults. A very primary and very serious task of child protection is to protect children from new abuses or further care failure. We should provide the children with the best security in meeting with new adults, who should be safe for them. By transposing the rules of police records on child-restraint services into child-restraint laws, better protection from abuse or serious harmful influence on minors is achieved. Childcare certificates are more extensive than the police certificates that you require today. Childcare certificates include comments related to sexual crime against adults, killings, drug crime and gross violence and criminality, as well as notes related to sexual crime against children. In addition, an exhaustive police certificate shall be required from foster parents and parents in the delivery centres. When you collect an exhaustive police statement, I think it's perfectly okay for you to open up to use the discretion of the less serious incidents, which would not be the result of a limited certificate. On the other hand, the Committee's plurality suggests that discretion cannot be used on a limited certificate, as it only shows the most serious aspects. I think we've seen serious and important examples lately that this shouldn't be based on judgement. On the limited certificates, we need clear rules to avoid doubt, for the benefit of children. The Progress Party proposes to require an exhaustive certificate also from support contacts and others who have similar and even contact with children. The Labour Party believes that this proposal is disproportionately strict, as a limited certificate will be covering. An exhaustive certificate covers all responses to offences against all prisons without a time limit. Less serious long-term gain crime will not necessarily give rise to doubt about the person’s property today. It is important to point out that the protection of children is provided by far more means in an institution than in a foster home or in a delivery home, and that it may be difficult in practice to choose the type of institutional settings for which an exhaustive certificate is to be required. Such a requirement is also, in our view, disproportionately strict control of the additions. In the new Police Register Act, it is open to demand a new police certificate attached to any criminal offences that have occurred since the inauguration. It is also important to see this in conjunction with the police’s duty to prevent crime. I would also like to show that, in connection with the treatment of police records laws, a proposal was discussed to require a new certificate every three years, as the Progress Party now proposes again. Only two years ago, it was agreed that such a home should not be included in the laws, but that instead, it should be decided to renew a background check in the case where the user of the information, for example: employers, have access to information which gives reason to believe that new information is of importance. The Ministry has argued that the renewal of background checks in the field of child protection must follow the appropriate demarcation. Nevertheless, we believe it is important that the Child Welfare Service has good internal procedures to assess the need for a new certificate of good conduct. The United Nations Convention on the Rights of the Child requires the State to ensure that the institutions and services responsible for the care or protection of children have the standard established, with particular regard to safety, health and the number of added and their qualifications as well as competent supervision. In my opinion, this is absolutely essential for our political work, and I am therefore very pleased with the important changes that are now being made in the Children's Protection Act.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-03-15.ana.ud2502e253	Child protection protects the most vulnerable children we have. This is a great responsibility. One of the measures to protect children in the field of child protection is to require a police record of persons working with the children. I am therefore pleased that the Committee joins in the proposal on the use of more extensive police certificates than today. The proposal is based on the rules on child care certificates in the Police Register Act, adopted last year. Such child care certificates shall show sexual assaults on children and adults, killings, gross violence and criminality and drug crimes. In accordance with the legislative proposal, all persons to be employed in the child protection service and in the institution shall provide such a certificate. I am also pleased that the Committee agrees with the proposal that such a certificate should also be required of persons who are not employed but who are to carry out tasks which bring about contact with children and young people. This will include, inter alia: relate to persons entrusted with tasks as part of relief measures or to institutions. This will contribute to increased trust in child protection. Obstetrical parents and private persons who accept children as relief measures shall provide an exhaustive police record of allegations related to offences against all offenders. This will result in better control of persons who have regular and over time responsibility for the care of minors. This is also in line with the rules of the Police Register Act which justify the use of an exhaustive certificate. However, I have noticed the minority's proposal that an exhaustive certificate should also be required of support contacts and others who have similar regular and close contact with children. The most difficult question in this case is the consequences of comments on a limited certificate of childcare. Several of the speakers have come up with that. Being excluded from child protection work is serious. There was considerable disagreement among the Hearing Officers as to whether there should be access to the use of discretion or not in the assessment of the consequences. The minority of the Committee states that there should be a rule of discretion. However, the legislative proposal does not allow for the use of judgement. This is, in particular, justified by the fact that the limited child-care certificate only shows infringements related to very serious circumstances. Furthermore, child protection is the responsibility of children in particularly vulnerable situations. Therefore, a judgement as to whether or not to employ a person with a comment should not be left to the individual employer. I'm glad the majority have joined this assessment. The exclusion will also be limited in time in the case of serious violence and criminality, as well as in the case of drug offences. However, in the case of an exhaustive certificate required by foster parents, judgement shall be used if there are allegations relating to offences against penalties other than those covered by a limited certificate. It will not always be such that a less serious crime from a long time ago — e.g. a win crime - makes a person unfit to be a foster parent. The assessment theme is also precisely whether the remark might raise doubts about his suitability. The requirement for a police record applies when employed. In some cases, there will be a need for re-control. This may be required by the employer if there is reason to believe that new material information is available. However, as the Storting assessed it in the treatment of the Police Register Act, subsequent routine checks on employees will be disproportionately resource-intensive. We have applied the equivalent in the area of child protection. However, it is important that child protection services develop good practices in order to ensure re-control where required. The changes in the child protection law adopted by the Stortinget today mean even better protection for the most vulnerable children and young people. It is the best thing for the child to weigh more heavily in these assessments.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-03-15.ana.ud2502e347	As the rapporteur on the matter, I would like to explain the Committee's views on the proposal, as well as that I would also like to say a little about the Christian People's Party's views on the issues concerned. I am pleased that the Committee in its position clearly stresses that the child’s best starting point for adoption and that it is the child’s best intention for adoption. The need for a family and for security is most important. There is no need for an adult to have a child who is the reason, starting point and purpose of the adoption process. The Committee stresses that there is a number of children who need a home and take into account the child’s waiting time, which must be controlling, not the number of adults who want to adopt or the waiting time of adults. The Committee also quotes in the opinion of the Advisory Committee on Childhood in the Norwegian debate: ‘The debate on adoption in Norway is too often marked by a perspective that focuses only on the wishes or demands of adults. This may concern either the original parents of the child or groups of applicants claiming ‘rights’ to adopt. It is important that all adoption should be assessed on the basis of the child’s needs and that adoption should only be used to the best of the child’s needs.’;» This is an important perspective, which we always have to remind ourselves of when we discuss adoption telematics. I am pleased that the Committee also stresses the seriousness of an adoption decision. Adoption is one of the most fundamental interventions the state can do to an individual. It is not only that the individual gets a new family, but also that he cuts over the legal ties to his original family. It is precisely the seriousness of the process that requires major procedural requirements, as well as standards of good repute, care, health, age, finances, housing, etc. The Committee therefore agrees with the proposers that a thorough evaluation of the adoption applicants is important. The Committee also agrees with the proposers that it should be an objective for Norwegian authorities to conclude new agreements with countries where necessary. The Committee points out that it is important for the Norwegian authorities to set high standards and to do a thorough job when approving new adoption countries. In its assessment of the proposed representative for NOU 2009, the Council of State refers to:21 Adoption – to the best of the child. The Committee agrees with the Council of State that the proposals contained in this draft representative must be seen in the context of the overall review of the field of adoption. The Committee assumes that the government will come to Stortinget with a broad review of the adoption field in a separate case and therefore awaits the further work of the Ministry. In view of the fact that document 8:6 for 2010-2011 is annexed to the Protocol. Without prejudging the general debate that is coming, I would say a little about the Christian People's Party's view. The Christian People's Party agrees that the adoption process can be simplified and take a shorter time. We particularly point in the attitude to the need to assess current age regulations and the practice of these provisions. The Christian People's Party agrees with the proposers that adoption aid should be increased. It is also the government parties in their party programmes, but not the Right Party and the Advance Party. The Christian People's Party has programmed and proposed a stepping up of adoption support to 2 G. The Christian People's Party has also proposed an increase of one-off support at birth and adoption to 2 G. With regard to the proposals put forward by the Left, I would say in the case of the Christian People's Party that we will not support those proposals when voting. It is not because we necessarily disagree with the proposals. We agree with the intentions of several of the proposals that are being promoted, but we respect the fact that the government is running a heavy process of reviewing a considerable amount of work that lies within that NOU, and we await the government to come back with a thorough review of the whole adoption field in which we can deal with the proposals from the Left in a wider context and give them the seriousness and thorough treatment that they deserve.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-03-15.ana.ud2502e380	The purpose of an adoption process is to give a good and lasting home to a child who cannot be taken care of by his biological parents. I think, in line with the hearing statement from the Adoption Forum, that the adoption debate in Norway is often marked by a lack of children's perspective, and that in fact we should note that in these debates. We don't talk about parents who have the right to children, but we talk about children who need a family. I agree that we need a further strengthening of the adoption field. It was not without reason that the government put down a public selection that would lead to a wide review of the adoption field. In line with what the proposers have to say and what the Council of State corresponds to the Committee, the Labour Party also believes that there are several good proposals here that are also involved in the adoption of the NOU – for the benefit of the child. I also think that we should be able to simplify the adoption processes, but not make them less safe. The purpose must always be to provide a good and lasting home for children. When this is clearly communicated, I will comment on this as the proposers deal with the waiting time and the process itself. My impression is that the waiting time, even after you are approved as an adoptive parent, can be long, for example. because several countries we have had a cooperation agreement with, are now being phased out. I think this is important to look at further, in line with what the proposers say in their proposal to conclude cooperation agreements with several countries. I would also like to point out that many of those who are currently going to deal with the start of adoption processes are the same people who are working on child protection issues in the municipalities, and therefore the government's strengthening of the municipal child protection area was also important in this context. For the Workers' Party, it is important to secure children, cf. The Children’s Convention, and we strongly disagree with the proposal of the Progress Party and the Right to facilitate the possibility of applying for adoption independently of countries that have been approved. For the Labour Party, it is important to ensure good agreements with countries from which we are to adopt, and we believe, in line with what a joint committee writes in its comments, that it is important that Norwegian authorities make high demands and do a thorough job when new adoption countries are to be approved. This is in stark contrast to the proposal from the Progress Party and the Right, and I want to be clear about what they actually mean here. The consequences we can see if you do not do a thorough job of approving adoption countries and adoption agencies are serious. The Hague Convention on International Adoption states that children should be protected against abduction, sale or trafficking. We can, among other things: look at several countries in Eastern Europe where children are heavily exposed to organised crime. The Council of Europe report, from the Social, Health and Family Committee, of 7 December 2007, points to a cruel example where children have been stolen from the birthplace and passed on to adoptive parents by corrupt health professionals who sell children to organised criminals — and the biological mother is told that the child was stillborn. This is actually happening in Eastern European countries today. For the Workers' Party, working against all forms of trafficking in human beings is absolutely crucial. We clearly believe that it is of decisive importance to maintain a strict requirement for both adoption intermediaries and the countries with which we conclude a cooperation agreement. This is one of the things. the reason Norway does not have a cooperation agreement with several countries in Eastern Europe. So my question is about the Right Party and the Progress Party is aware of the consequences of its proposal to make it possible to apply for adoption from non-approved countries. At least the working party takes a distance from such a proposal. I am pleased that the debate on adoption and adoption processes is about more than just the adoption benefit. This debate is about more than just a single budget line. But I also notice that the Right wants to increase the adoption benefit without having coverage for this in its alternative budget. The purpose of this process is to provide a child with a safe and sustainable home. At the same time, there is, in fact, more demand for young, healthy children. The sad paradox in international adoption is that there are many children between the ages of five and 15 who need a new home, and many families are looking for children to take care of, but they want the little ones. A shorter waiting time for the searchers must never go beyond their children, never go beyond a thorough and good evaluation of the searchers. Therefore, it is so unlikely that it is important for the Labour Party that we have secure and good cooperation agreements with the countries from which we are to adopt, and that we are able to do something with any unnecessary bureaucracy without jeopardising the legal security of children or the best of children.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-03-15.ana.ud2502e498	The staging representatives Trine Skei Grande and Abid Q. Raja proposes to make the adoption process more predictable and less bureaucratic. I agree with the representatives that the adoption process should be as predictable as possible and that there should be no more procedure than necessary. The question, however, is how the balance between predictable and flexible legislation should be. Clear requirements for applicants must be met, but the applicant’s personal suitability to adopt a child will always have to weigh more heavily. I respect Trine Skei Grande's defence for the ideal motives, but we do not get away with any judgements that need to be made. These assessments must exercise professional judgement. Important considerations must be made on the basis of what is best for the child. We want a better adoption process. For this reason, in 2008 the government reduced a public sample that has gone through the adoption field. The investigation has been widely consulted and the ministry is now following up the investigation and the consultation opinions. In this work we will look at the issues addressed by the representatives in their Document 8 proposals. Adoption is primarily about giving a child who needs it, a good and lasting home. This is underlined in the UN Convention on the Rights of the Child and the Hague Convention on International Adoption. All 83 countries have joined the Hague Convention, including Norway. Therefore, the essential interests of the child must be safeguarded in the adoption process. I am pleased that the Committee also draws up the child perspective in its position. The proposal of the representatives of the European Parliament, like the Committee on Adoption, aims to simplify, streamline and enhance the quality of the adoption work. Since we are now in a process – and it is active, I can assure you – where we look again in the field, it is difficult for me to give concrete answers to the representatives. However, I would like to mention some key proposals. The committee proposes to transfer the investigation of adoption applicants from the municipality to the five regions of the Children’s, Youth and Family Catastrophe. Its purpose is, among other things: shortening the procedure time. Key requirements for applicants, such as age, health, housing, economy and habits and the duration of marriage, want to make the sample more precise and much less discretionary. They suggest, for example: to reduce the requirement to the age of applicants from 45 to 42 years, with a few exceptions. According to the sample, the requirement for health needs to be standardised to a large extent and they propose the development of a guide specifying some key and typical diagnoses, diseases and disabilities that lead to rejection of the application. Here I find that the committee's proposals differ somewhat from the views of the storage representatives in the present proposals. They signal a desire for a less rigid code. This shows that there are different views as to which criteria should apply and to what extent discretion should be left to the authorities. These are just examples of sample proposals that we are now looking at. As regards the actual treatment of adoptive applicants, I stress that this should be done in a good way. Applicants shall be taken into account in the adoption process. The children’s, youth and family budgets are committed to this and have taken initiatives to ensure sound and sound procedures. These will be implemented quickly and not wait for the overall review. Developments in the country of origin of children affect international cooperation on adoption. Countries of origin aim to prioritise national adoptions rather than foreign adoptions. This is done at the same time as several beneficiary countries wish to establish cooperation with the countries concerned. Some countries of origin therefore receive far more applications than children are available for adoption. Treatment time is increasing in these countries. The waiting time, for example: in China is currently over four and a half years. There are many orphans in the world who may need to be adopted abroad. But at the same time, there are some countries that cannot or do not want to cooperate on international adoption, for various reasons. In several countries, adoption of children abroad is a highly sensitive and controversial issue. In other countries, the situation is characterised by instability, for example: in the event of war and natural disasters. I am concerned that the organisations are given the opportunity to cooperate on international adoption in countries where there are both children who need to be adopted abroad and where there is a wish from the authorities. However, the country has a regulatory framework and a sound system for the dissemination of adoption. We must prevent the activity from being exploited by someone for the abduction and trafficking of children. When cooperating with a new country, the Norwegian authorities must make a thorough assessment before granting an authorisation. Representatives propose that adoption organisations should be given the opportunity to carry out probationary adoptions in a country prior to granting a final brokerage authorisation. This is already being done. For example, I can. mention that in January of this year the Directorate authorised the carrying out of test cases from Mali. In addition to this, issues related to international cooperation will naturally be at home in the review we are now conducting. I am pleased that the committee is concerned that the government will come back to Stortinget with a broad review of this important field in a matter of its own. This, of course, will be met.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-03-15.ana.ud2502e641	I would like to address with this interpellation a debate here in the United Kingdom on what ambitions Norway can have and should have for its leading knowledge institutions. In recent years we have seen strong international competition for resources for research and knowledge development, both in terms of human and financial resources. This concerns aspects such as international research cooperation, e.g.: within the EU, where the road choices and priorities made by others become increasingly important for Norway. How attractive Norway is as a partner country depends on the quality of our researchers and institutions, what working conditions we can offer, but also on what resources we ourselves can prioritise to support our best research environments. For universities, we see an increasingly strong international competition, including for students. Norway has undoubtedly among the best conditions in the world purely financially for students, but as a small country on the outskirts of the world, it is not unnatural that larger and more prestigious universities in larger countries beat us in the competition for the best talents. More relationships can work in here, but it's not unnatural to believe that the international rankings we've got in the last few years become more important. In good Norwegian academic tradition, many in the sector devalue these rankings and claim to be faulty or irrelevant. Sometimes skepticism may seem to increase if one’s own institution comes out poorly — and it may be human. At the last Times Higher Education ranking last year, the University of Bergen and the University of Oslo joined among the 200 leading universities in the world. Unlike before, the University of Oslo had to see itself this time turned off by the University of Bergen, which ended up in 135th place. The position implies that the University of Bergen is now ranked as No. 6 of the universities in the Nordic countries. It is difficult to determine whether these are results we should be satisfied or dissatisfied with. We look at the world's leading universities, for example. They are almost without exception larger than ours, have longer history and are more well-known, have more resources and are located in larger countries, such as the United States and Japan. USA, France, United Kingdom, Canada, Japan, etc. For example, the foundation behind the world's best university, Harvard, is considered to be the world's second richest organization, after the Catholic Church. So there's formidable competitors that our universities have to measure against. But even though it may seem unreasonable to be compared with such strong competitors, competition and ranking are still a fact to which we have to deal. The answer to the question of whether it is good, or too bad with the best Norwegian locations, is essentially a question of what level of ambition we as a nation want for our best universities. Other questions, too, it is important to seek answers, may be how realistic it is to achieve significantly higher placements, which factors we can most easily influence in that direction, and what resources it will require. The University of Oslo has recently presented its strategy for the next ten years. The level of ambition is to raise quality and become more relevant and visible internationally. The University of Bergen is in the middle of the process of its new strategy. In the course of this work, I have registered some disagreements about objectives, ambitions and priorities. A fundamental question that seems to go back in such discussions is whether it is right to prioritize something in front of something else, in other words whether there should be emphasis on point over width. It's a discussion that I also want to address in this interview, but then in a more national perspective. I therefore believe that the nation of Norway should have an opinion on what ambitions we are going to have for our leading universities. Our leading researchers and research communities are at the top of the world in their fields, and there's no reason for our best universities not to be the same. However, as already mentioned, several competitors have a formidable position, and it is therefore important to be realistic. A goal that can be ambitious and realistic can, for example: be that the foremost Norwegian university shall be ranked as the best in the Nordic region by 2020. Today, the University of Bergen, as best in Norway – as I mentioned earlier – is no. 6 in the North. What that will require of the universities and of the others, I don't have the answer. But it should be possible for a rich and well-educated country like Norway to have such ambitions. Work must start with an analysis and then a decision on objectives, and then a binding agreement must be reached between the leading universities and the Ministry of Knowledge on actions and funding. It would then be natural to take the strategic documents of universities and to develop their strongest environments further. The best university in the Nordic region is the Karolinska Institutet in Sweden today, in position no. 43. Should the University of Bergen, for example, go up to this level, it means to climb approx. 90 places. It would certainly require resources and systematic quality development. But the gain will not only be a better ranking. A stronger investment in our leading universities is just what is happening, among other things. the central bank manager now calls for a higher level of investment in future value added.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-03-15.ana.ud2502e667	There is always a great deal of attention in the media about the position of Norwegian universities on international rankings. The two most famous are Times Higher Education Supplement and Shanghai Jiao Tong University. Universities are important social institutions that deserve a large place in public space, which the international rankings contribute to. But in order to have a reflected approach to rankings, it is important to know what is actually being measured, what criteria are being taken into account, and what emphasis are being given to the different criteria. Many ranks measure only parts of the activity at the institutions — often just research. These activities can be measured by selecting certain criteria, such as: the number of Nobel Prize winners the institution has fostered, or one can rely on subjective assessments of the reputation of universities. In addition, some of the rankings have an impact on science and medicine. The indicators used thus highlight a limited part of the activity. The consequence is that many and important aspects of university activities are not used in the assessment basis in the most well-known rankings. Education and training are given little weight and many disciplines are not included in the calculation basis. Because the most profiled rankings do not distinguish between disciplines, the results may mask major differences in quality within universities. It's rarely that a width university is best in all areas. For potential students and other stakeholders, the quality of each subject may be more important than the overall outcome of the apprenticeship. Alternative rankings have therefore been developed based on excellent disciplines rather than excellent universities. These have so far received less attention. It's usually the same universities that get to the top of the most famous rankings. The Shanghai and Times rankings favor traditional and research-intensive universities in English-speaking countries. If we ignore the English-speaking, it's actually so that Norwegian and Nordic universities come out well. The institutions most often placed at the top of the ranking list also have the prerequisites and framework conditions of the few other universities, including: access to large donations, funds and land assets. Furthermore, we have seen that some of the rankings are constantly changing indicators and methods of calculation, which can make the results unpredictable. This has led to large fluctuations in placement from one year to the next, although there is rarely any significant change in quality in professional activity within such a short period of time. Unilateral attention to rankings may lead to uniformity because institutions will adapt to the criteria to score high on them. In order to counteract uniformity, alternatives have been developed to rankings, alternatives aimed at presenting and promoting the diversity of the sector. A European initiative that looks at the opportunity to make diversity more visible is the so-called U-map initiative. The typology is multidimensional and does not aim at ranking; it is intended to make the landscape more visible among higher education institutions. The classification system is based on 14 dimensions, such as: education, research, innovation, international orientation and cultural and regional engagement. Inspired by this, in its annual state report for universities and colleges, the Ministry of Knowledge has developed a classification system to present the profile of each university or university. This is a non-hierarchical approach designed to raise awareness and trigger reflections on the nature of institutions. This work has received good feedback from the sector and is something the ministry will develop further. In addition to the objective of having a diverse landscape for higher education, we obviously need to have high ambitions for our universities and colleges. It is also apparent from the Law of the University and the Higher Education Act, which states that universities are to ‘contribute to Norwegian higher education and research following the international research front’. As a small country, Norway cannot be the best in everything, but we must nevertheless assert ourselves in some areas. Among other things, we do. set up centres for excellent research. 18 out of 21 SSFs are affiliated to universities. An evaluation of the scheme last year showed that it has been a success and helped to make Norwegian research internationally visible. In addition to having research environments at the top of the world, I am also very concerned about education quality. I have expectations that universities and colleges will provide high-quality research-based education. In order to stimulate quality in education, it is, among other things: to set up a system for centres of excellence in education. I have great expectations for this effort. The government sees the need for more robust professional environments and contributes to this by stimulating increased cooperation, division of labour and professional concentration within and between institutions. The government budget allocated SAK funds for both 2010 and 2011, and we are now seeing several promising projects as a result of these incentives. The majority of funds are allocated to major regional cooperation processes involving several institutions. The aim of these actions is to increase the quality of education, research and dissemination, thus strengthening the institutions as international knowledge actors. The efforts made in both research and education show that the list is being high and that we are going to have excellent environments in different parts of universities' activities. We can also see the results of the bets. Firstly, there has been a strong growth in the publication of Norwegian researchers. Measured in the number of research articles per 1 000 inhabitants, Norway is fifth place in the world. Secondly, Norwegian research is being quoted more and more. While scientific publication and citation are established indicators of research quality, similar developments for education have been less developed. A quality aspect of higher education is the benefits of training in relation to the demands of the labour market. Norwegian higher education seems to provide a good starting point for professional life. A study shows that candidates from Norwegian universities are far more satisfied with how education prepared them for work than were candidates from 12 other countries in this comparison. However, there is a need for increased knowledge of the quality of education in Norway. We are therefore part of an international project, the AHELO project, which aims to compare students' learning outcomes across countries. The way students perceive the quality is captured to some extent in various student studies. However, such studies have so far been little systematic and scientific. The Ministry of Knowledge therefore assesses the need for a national student survey to improve knowledge of student assessments of quality and learning outcomes. Figures from Eurostudents show that Norwegian students are among those who receive fewer lessons in Europe. In addition, evaluations and reaccreditations from NOKUT have pointed to weaknesses in several courses. We regularly hear about reduced group education, lack of follow-up and feedback to the students, poor management of the study programmes, etc. This underlines the importance of monitoring the content of education and the quality of study together with the institutions that have the formal responsibility. It also underlines the importance of continuing pressure on the work of better management at our universities and colleges. The fact that institutions too set themselves high targets is reflected in their strategy papers: The University of Oslo has as its main ambition ‘to develop UiO into an international top university’. The University of Bergen’s goal is ‘to further develop UiB as an internationally visible research university’. NTNU has given its strategy the title ‘NTNU 2020 — International excellence’. In total, this shows that we have universities with high ambitions. The Government's objective is that Norwegian research and higher education should be of a high international class. For the government, it is not an end in itself that Norwegian universities should climb the rankings. We believe that a wide range of instruments are necessary to measure international quality. On the other hand, international rankings have come to be, and they measure parts of the activity of our schools. It would therefore be natural to draw attention to the development of the rankings in order to improve the quality of research and higher education. The government's research and higher education objectives are reported to the Stortinget through outlays and budget documents. Several of the issues relevant to Warloe's question have been notified to Stortinget in my period as the Council of State. The Storting Communication on Internationalisation and the Storing Communication on Research explains the government's international ambitions for Norwegian research and higher education. There are no plans for an analysis for universities separately. I believe that the role of universities internationally in research and higher education is best analysed in a comprehensive way. Finally, I would like to mention that the government has pointed out this year, 2011, to the Year of Science. We will invite you to discuss the importance of higher education and research for growth, well-being and cultural development, as well as the fact that there is also a very important discussion within and outside the academic environment. These will be important discussions that will give direction to what the leading professional communities will be after in the years to come.

Therefore, I have to say that I am actually a little skeptical about the government's and SV's research time counting project. As one researcher said in the Research Forum, it's sometimes the time you don't research, that the thoughts of the most important research actually come. It is important sometimes to count gaps between research in order to count the quality of research. That's what I think I was wise to say. It's also a thought for us who think it's exciting to read scientific history, about the great scientists and the great scientists in our scientific history. You can wonder how they fit into our counting systems, in our rankings and in our institutions today. Had we managed to develop that kind of quality? Had we managed to develop the kind of research personalities that really contribute to the quantum leap of science in our structures today? I'm not so sure. But then to what's left's honor. If we're going to be able to achieve the goals we have, we're seriously concerned when the basic appropriations for the institutions are being pushed over time, as we now see them do. What basic appropriations are about is academic freedom. It is a matter of maritime administrations taking the kind of priority choices that institutions have to make, which are as close to scientists as they actually are. We are also concerned that we are now seeing places of study being increasingly underfunded, resulting in pressure on basic appropriations, and pressure on the quality of institutions. All of this helps to push the institutions into the ability to achieve the quality we want them to have. Maybe it's the fight for increased basic funding, the fight for academic freedom, and making sure that colleges are properly funded, which are some of the main things we can do if we want to bring our scientific environments up to a higher level.

Article 9 of the Convention on Human Rights states that everyone has the right “to express his religion or belief by worship, teaching, practice and observance either alone or together with others, both publicly and privately”. In the second term, the following is added: ‘The freedom to express their religion or belief shall be subject only to such restrictions as are prescribed by law and necessary in a democratic society for reasons of public security, for the protection of public order, health or morality, or for the protection of the rights and freedoms of others.’;» So, your freedom of religion doesn't stop when it violates mine. The community will intervene if your religious practice or practice violates Norwegian law. Does the Progress Party believe that those who wear hijab threaten the religion of others? In Norway we will follow the basic principles of freedom of religion and life and non-discrimination. The Department of Law of the Ministry of Justice has assessed what a possible ban on hijab will mean. They indicate that, in some cases, the European Court of Human Rights has accepted a ban on hijab in some countries. However, these decisions relate to countries with a fundamental fundamental distinction between state and religion. In Norway, we do not have a clear distinction between state and religion, as I have explained earlier. Therefore, there is little doubt that in Norway there is a ban on hijab in violation of human rights. I believe that the Progress Party is concerned with integration – to a certain extent at least. But in what way does the Progress Party believe that the proposal for a ban on hijab in primary school contributes to integration? A ban on hijab at school may, on the contrary, lead to more Muslims sending their children to their own schools where hijab is allowed. Therefore, such a ban may help to prevent rather than promote integration. In addition, I would like to know why the Progress Party will not ban other religious symbols on children, such as the Sikhs' Turban or the Calot of the Jews. What about Islam or Muslims that the Progress Party has such a strong need to regulate?

[[EMK]] I have read the verdict, and after doing that, I wonder if the Progress Party has done the same thing. The judgment is about three women who in 1995 went to a pharmacy in France to buy prescription contraceptives. They were met by Bruno Pichon and Marie-Line Sajous who owned and operated the pharmacy. These two refused to sell the funds on the grounds that it was against their religious beliefs. In its assessment, the EMD points out that Article 9 is primarily about personal conviction and individual conscience and protects actions closely linked to the type of issues such as: «acts of defence forming part of the practice of a religion or a purpose in a generally accepted form». That the use of hijab in general is to be regarded as religious practice is covered by Article 9. It has been pointed out by the Ministry of Justice in its review of the established case-law of the EMD. So this quote is in the sentence that the Party of Advancement pushes in front of it: «However, in safety this personal domain, Article 9 of the Convention does not always guarantee the right to remain in public in a manner governed by that purpose.» What the Court has done is first to explain the scope of Article 9, then the limitations in it. The Party of Progress has quoted the restrictions without quoting the scope. To all intents and purposes, the restrictions put forward by the EMD have been quoted when the Court is discussing a matter which is in no way comparable to what their proposal for a representative is about. In other words, the legal basis for this comment from the Progress Party is so infantile that even a law student in the first department would have joined the reading room all the way to the reading room if he had been exposed to this.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-03-15.ana.ud2502e1797	The kindergarten sector has become, in a few years, a major and important sector of the welfare community. About 270,000 children and 85 000 employees have their daily work in the nursery. Today, we have approached full child care coverage, so why is further development of kindergarten still so high on the political agenda? Well, there's a lot of reasons for that. Because even though we have come very far in the kindergarten field in Norway — yes, so far that I am almost willing to make the claim that Norway is sovereign, Norway is in elite division with regard to our kindergartens — we still have some challenges in kindergarten. Many countries come here to copy our kindergarten concept. There is a confirmation of the status of Norwegian kindergartens internationally. Nevertheless, we can get even better, and I feel that many people have a focus on the content of kindergartens. Politicians, both local and national, as well as key social actors in the sector, are concerned with the focus on quality development. In the light of that, I am very happy to have the opportunity to discuss the content of children's kindergarten everyday with the Minister of Knowledge, who is one of the politicians who should have boasted too much of what has happened in the kindergarten field in recent years, a politician who has a great commitment to Norwegian kindergartens. Kindergarten is an important part of child rearing in Norway. Many children spend many hours in kindergarten during a week. For the right, the most important thing in the kindergarten debate is to keep the child in focus. Right wants to preserve and develop the best of our kindergarten tradition, where children are allowed to be children, where childhood has a pronounced self-esteem, and where children meet with respect as whole people. Right wants to take care of the peculiarity of kindergarten by focusing on the fact that childhood and kindergarten are a learning and preparation for what comes later in life. For us in the Right, it is important to stress that children should have children first and foremost when they are in kindergarten – play and learning go hand in hand. The right goal is to have good kindergartens as the ability to see play and learning together with well-being and the creation of children. All children are different, and so we don't think there is any phase in how this is going to happen, and the way that good learning is going to take place in kindergarten. The lack of kindergartens and nursery services shows this in practice. There is a diversity we in the Right are concerned with preserving and facilitating in our further work on kindergarten. All children shall have the possibility of a high-quality childcare facility, regardless of whether they are in a private or municipal nursery. But that is what we are going to do in this debate. It is essential to get more knowledge about the quality of kindergartens and how we can improve quality. To put it simply, having cheap kindergartens does not help if the quality of the kindergartens is not the goal. For although quality in kindergarten is defined by many different factors, such as staff, annual plans, follow-up of daily routines, area and much more, the Right believes that the main player, which ensures a good quality day of nursery care, is the employees. Safe, clear and caring adults with whom children can relate are important. Well, we want them to have stable adults around them who can take care of them, who see our kids, who can provide them with security and care. In the right, we have proposed to strengthen the quality of kindergartens with additional means to create a ‘recruitment strategy’ for preschool teachers in kindergartens and to strengthen the work on continuing education. We have proposed to strengthen research in kindergartens, and we have also proposed to create a project for pre-school teacher education, à la GNIST, which is a project that provides good results in school. ParlaMint-NO_2011-03-15.ana.ud2502e2078	Norwegian business is dominated by small businesses. A large proportion of these are family businesses. This structure suggests that there must be a major focus on the fact that we have a regulatory framework adapted to these small businesses. Policies that facilitate the establishment and operation of an establishment will result in increased employment, growth and ability to value the country and thus contribute to ensuring public and private well-being. The proposers justify the proposal with a desire to make the form of a limited liability company more accessible to traders, as the current capital requirements for the start-up of a public limited liability company are perceived as an obstacle. The requirement today is a share capital of at least DKK 100000. The Ministry of Justice’s letter of reply refers to the fact that investigations are currently being carried out with a view to simplifying the share law. Reduced capital requirements are among the themes addressed. The Council of State therefore refers to the examination of this investigation after it has been consulted. In the proceedings of the Judicial Committee, the parties to the Government also refer to this investigation and the ongoing work on it. You propose that the draft representative be annexed to the Minutes. The Progress Party and the Right propose that the Share Company Act be amended by reducing the minimum share capital to NOK 25 000. For the Right, this is an expression that we want simplified rules for business and lower thresholds to establish our own business. A study presented by the NHO shows that almost half of Norwegian limited liability companies are registered with NOK 100000 in share capital and that the same applies to 70 pst. by newly established limited liability companies in 2010. Thus, a large number of limited liability companies have invested at a level with the lowest possible share capital. In addition, a reduction in the capital requirement can be expected to offset the differences to, and reduce, the number of Norwegian registered Foreign Enterprises, so-called NUFs. The requirement for a certain sized share capital is historically based on, inter alia, the need for: to protect creditors. Today, however, it is pointed out that the creditor’s protection is, instead, linked to much more stringent requirements for correct information. For many potential entrepreneurs, the requirement for a share capital of at least DKK 100 000 is a major obstacle. Modern communication and information registers enable creditors to secure themselves in ways other than by looking only at the share capital. This is a view supported by several sources, including: by the NHO. In Sweden, the minimum share capital for private limited liability companies was reduced to SEK 50000 from 1 April 2010. Together with new rules for voluntary audits, it is pointed out that this is one of the main reasons for the strong increase in the number of newly established companies in Sweden. This is a development Right-wing want to facilitate also here in Norway. That is why we are promoting the Committee's minority proposals from the Progress Party and the Right.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-03-15.ana.ud2502e2099	The representative Anders B. Werp has taken up the proposal he referred to.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-03-15.ana.ud2502e2102	In June 2010, the Ministry of Justice commissioned Mr. Gudmund Knudsen to settle questions about simplification of the share law. The mandate dealt with rules on the formation of limited liability companies, capital rules and rules relating to organisational issues. Later, representatives from the Progress Party have put forward a proposal for a representative to reduce capital requirements for limited liability companies. Advocate Knudsen presented his Opinion on the proposal to amend the Share Law on 7 January 2011, and the matter is now pending. The Judicial Committee's position shows that there is now a broad cross-political consensus on the issue of share capital requirements in the Share Act. I would like to remind you that Soria Moria II also stressed the need to put in place simpler rules for business. Lawyer Knudsen has made suggestions to lower the minimum share capital of a share company from today’s NOK 100000 to NOK 30000. In other words, it should be cheaper to set up a limited liability company, he thinks. The proposal by Mr Knudsen, and this proposal for a representative that we are discussing today, have been seen as an important input into the government's simplification debate. The proposals are also important in order to harmonise our requirements with our neighbouring countries. Furthermore, as many claim, it can be a good measure to curb NUF, i.e. Norwegian-registered Foreign Enterprises. Other elements that are important to discuss are whether any reduced minimum share capital will have some meaning for more start-ups, whether it would weaken the protection of creditors, or whether a change could help to increase the risk of loss or fraud — in other words, financial crime. So there are several elements that need to be carefully assessed before reducing any capital requirement. Some also call for a full removal of the requirement of share capital. I don't think that makes sense. I'm probably afraid that such a grip would open up to several unprofessional actors, and that little thoughtful establishment would then become a reality. I clearly see many great and good advantages in reducing the requirement for share capital in the stock laws. Creating a limited liability company will be cheaper. One gets a harmonisation with our neighboring country. It will deflect against the NUF, and it will have meaning for new start-ups. The government will soon decide on this issue and other elements of the stock laws, as advocate Knudsen has suggested. I would of course be waiting for the hearing results, and I believe that it is urgent to get a clarification. Although minimum share capital requirements should be seen in conjunction with other elements of the share law, I nevertheless believe that it should be assessed whether the share capital requirement can be assessed separately and brought forward quickly before the Storting – this in order to make the form of a share company even more popular, so that one might also be able to cope with NUF establishment. And so we're going to have a little bit more time to wind up these establishments. I look forward to a hearing response and, in the light of what I have just said, I would like to recommend the Committee's plural position.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-03-15.ana.ud2502e2199	First, I would like to say that I am pleased that there is a broad consensus to consider the requirement for share capital in the Share Act. Before the proposal for a representative was presented, the government had started work such as: the capital requirement of limited liability companies. A central part of the mission was to assess whether the minimum capital requirement should be lowered. The investigation was carried out on 7 January of this year and is now under consultation, as already mentioned, with a deadline of 19 April of this year. Lawyer Knudsen has proposed that the minimum share capital requirement under the Share Law should be NOK 30 000. The investigation also promotes other important proposals for simplification and modernisation of the Shares Act, including: simpler rules for the formation of limited liability companies and rules giving companies greater freedom to choose how to organise themselves. Some decorations are also proposed in the rules on credit to own shareholders and the acquisition of own shares by the company. The last proposal may in particular have an impact on the implementation of generational shifts and facilitate the participation of workers in the company. Several have asked whether the proposal on the size of the share capital can be dealt with out of step and faster than the remainder of the selection. However, the size of the share capital will affect a number of the other rules of the law. This means that the requirement for share capital must be seen in conjunction with other rules of the Share Act and not assessed in isolation. The form of the limited liability company is the most widely used form of business in Norway and therefore the rules of the Share Act have a major impact on traders. Moreover, the main emphasis of limited liability companies is small and medium-sized enterprises, and it is therefore particularly important to look at simplifications for these companies. The government's work on the Shares Act follows up the Soria Moria II Declaration, which explicitly mentioned business simplification, in particular for small and medium-sized enterprises. The simplification and modernisation of the Share Act must also be seen in the context of other proposals and measures by the government. In particular, I mention the proposal to grant an exemption from the audit obligation for small limited liability companies, which will be dealt with in the April Stortinget. Overall, these two proposals represent opportunities for significant simplifications and savings for limited liability companies and contribute to the competitiveness of Norwegian rules. In addition, I also point to the possibilities for electronic reporting through Altin and further work on this. It is also announced that the government will present a numerical target to reduce the costs to businesses and propose concrete measures and plans to achieve this objective. There have been 200 000 new jobs in the private sector in the last ten years, and business, including entrepreneurs, has good jobs in Norway. The government attaches great importance to the simplification work to benefit entrepreneurs in this country.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-03-15.ana.ud2502e2243	Most people agree that criminal acts and violent behaviour must be dealt with by the community. However, in meeting young offenders under the age of authority, it is not only a matter of punishment, it must also be a matter of rehabilitation. There must be an objective to benefit from individual follow-up schemes with control and focus on changing undesirable behaviour. It is easier to reverse adverse developments when actions are initiated early. Of course, children and young people who commit serious criminal acts, as reported in the media, and to which the proposers refer, must be protected and taken care of so that they do not cause serious harm to others. In this area, we have a major challenge, and I would say that we have been a little too short, and that we lack good enough tools and offers. Child protection is contacted and comes in when you're dealing with a criminal youth under the age of 18. In some particularly challenging and difficult cases, it is not possible to avoid a criminal reaction in the form of prisons with custody and housing, and it is not a good thing for these young people, because then they often get together with older and heavier criminals. The Children’s Protection Act allows for the forced placing of children and young people with serious behavioural problems in the field of child protection. It is the councils for child protection and social matters that take decisions in these cases, cf. the Child Protection Act. The proposers believe that the legal certainty of those who are being forced against their will is not sufficiently ensured when a county body determines such a location. The proposers believe that a court should take decisions when children and young people are placed under compulsion against their own consent or the consent of the courts. The Committee seems to have raised a very important issue and shared the concerns of the proposers regarding measures and follow-up of young people who have landed on the slope. It is a fact that there are often not enough prevention measures when school, relatives and networks are concerned about young people who have started to change behaviour and who are moving in troubled environments with drugs and crime. Young people who have started a criminal career need to be met with clarity by society, with proper follow-up, and with criminal reactions that seem rehabilitating. The Committee also agrees that children and young people under the age of 18 should not be penalised in ordinary prisons together with older sentences and detention centres. Such an impact on older and heavier criminals could reinforce the unfortunate development of young people. The Committee underlines and points out that children and young people with criminal behaviour need a safe and firm framework and a structured approach that can help them to move on to a new track. In this context, the Committee will refer to pilot projects already under way. A youth unit is established in Bergen, and one here is also planned in the eastern region. These are special prison units under criminal law. These units are for young people aged 15 to 18 years and should be able to accommodate young people from all over the country. It shall be an offer both to custody inmates and to courts. The units in their design shall be similar to a non-incarcerated dwelling and shall be arranged in such a way that family and family members can have contact with their children. The safety requirement and the specific needs of the team shall be addressed by a combination of building and manning measures. As far as staff are concerned, half of the staff have a background in prison. The remaining have backgrounds in environmental therapy, pedagogics and psychiatry. The majority of the Committee, the members of the Labour Party, the Right, the Socialist Left Party and the Central Party, believe that alternative forms of response to prison should be used as far as possible, so that imprisonment can only be used once all other options have been evaluated. Norway is obliged by the United Nations Convention on the Rights of the Child, which points out that imprisonment of children is only to be used as a last resort and for the shortest possible period of time. Sometimes, unfortunately, the drastic step must be taken to bring about a deterrent response to children, too, in the interests of social protection. This is the case where children and young people have committed particularly serious or repeated crimes. The majority of the Committee would also point out that the government has announced that it will make a legislative proposal with a notification part to be completed this spring, which will address the overall fight against childhood and juvenile delinquency. The majority of the Committee does not support the proposal No. 1, where they pray ‘The Government shall bring an action before the Storting for a change in child protection practices, where such decisions are taken by a court rather than by the county’. The majority in the Committee is of the opinion that it is too detailed to go into the field. The majority believe that replacing a county court hearing requires a more thorough professional assessment and investigation. The members of the Committee from the Right support the intention of the proposal by the proposers and refer to the lack of offers to young people committing repeated criminal acts, and the powerless situation of child protection sometimes exists in dealing with some younger offenders with major problems of violence and crime. I address the following suggestions from the Right: ‘The government is asking the Government to promote the cause of the Stortinget with the necessary legislative changes to ensure a better follow-up of young people who have started a criminal racetrack.’

ParlaMint-NO_2011-03-15.ana.ud2502e2354	In the 2005 Soria Moria Declaration, the government announced a review of the situation of convictions under 18 years of age. The case was dealt with in St.meld. No 20 for 2005–06, Alternative criminal responses to young offenders, followed through NOU 2008:15 Children and punishment – development aid and control. The government's aim is not to put children in prison. The government will therefore continue its efforts to increase the use of alternative forms of reaction and criminal enforcement. Prison shall only be used once all other alternatives have been evaluated. The United Nations Convention on the Rights of the Child, by which Norway is obliged, points out that imprisonment of children should only be used as a last resort and for a minimum period of time. However, the importance of social protection makes it necessary to have a detention response available to children who have committed serious or repeated crimes. In these cases, the government is concerned that the content of the sentence is of high rehabilitation quality and that children under the age of 18 do not live with older convictions and custody prisoners. In Norway, fortunately, we have few children in prison, at any time approx. 10 persons under 18 years of age. For these children, the government has decided to set up two distinct youth units under criminal law as a pilot project. The units shall receive youth from all over the country and be an offer to both caretakers and referees. So far, one youth unit is established in connection with Bergen prison. The unit has been in operation since 1 September 2009 in temporary premises and has a multidisciplinaryly composed crew of 18 persons in turn. There is also a psychologist added to the device, such as have tasks related to the screening and individual treatment of prisoners and to the guidance of officials. The government is working on the establishment of a similar unit in the eastern part of the country. The units in their design shall be approximately equal to a dwelling without a prison term. Provision shall be made for family or family members to have contact with their children. The safety requirement and the specific needs of the team shall be addressed by a combination of building and manning measures. The government is working for good cross-border cooperation arrangements that provide children with a well-coordinated and multidisciplinary offer both while in prison and after their release. In that NOU on children and punishment, alternative responses and responses to serious juvenile delinquency were further investigated. In order to reduce the number of children in prison, it is proposed to introduce a new criminal reaction — the major youth meeting — as an alternative to unconditional imprisonment. The target group is children under the age of 18 who have committed repeated and/or serious crime. In the new reaction, social control through close follow-up will replace the physical control of prison. During the spring session, the government will promote a legislative proposal that follows up the proposals of the Children and Punishment Investigation. The legislative proposal shall address the overall fight against childhood and juvenile delinquency, involving changes in, inter alia: the criminal procedure law, the criminal law, the conflict resolution law and the criminal enforcement law. The representatives' proposals for changing child protection practices have been submitted to the Ministry of Children and Gender Equality, which states that the role of the county councils has been assessed several times since it was established in 1993. In St.meld. No 23 for 2000–2001 by the Ministry of Justice, it is noted that the advantages of county councillors over ordinary courts are that the county councillors have acquired a wide range of experience and specific competences in the field of child protection, while at the same time working under the same basic rules applicable to the courts. In 2006, Stortinget adopted new rules of procedure for its members. Their aim is to ensure uniform and appropriate procedures in the counties in line with the basic rules of good practice applicable in the ordinary courts. In conclusion, I would like to point out that there is a broad consensus in the Committee on the proposal to set up separate prisons for young people aged 15 to 18, both to improve the content of the compensation and to avoid children under 18 being in ordinary prisons together with older sentences and care services. The majority of the Committee, the members of the Labour Party, the Right, the SV and the Central Party, support the government’s objective of using alternative forms of response to prison as far as possible, so that prison is used only after all other alternatives have been evaluated. The majority do not support the proposal to change the practice of placing children in child protection, since, in the view of the majority, it is too detailed to go into the field.

This will enable all graduate police high school students to get a job in the police department. The police director can inform that by 10 March this year, 372 of the 428 graduates of high school police have been working in the police from this fall. Of these, 390 have passed the exam. According to the Police Directorate, there are now 216 vacant positions in the Police and National Staff Committee, of which 75 are police officers. I have noted the comments made by the majority of the Judicial Committee on the draft document 8 by the local government representatives, Mr Werp, Mr Aspaker and Mr Dahl on the plan for ICT investment in the police sector and the real strengthening of the police operating budget. The majority refer to the work already being done by the police in designing and implementing new ICT infrastructure, improving information security and preparing for the renewal of criminal justice systems. The majority also refers to the specific funding of the ICT initiative in 2010 and 2011, of 180 million, respectively. NOK 250 and SEK 250 million. kr. Furthermore, the majority consider that there is a need to close the project before it can be concluded in view of the final investment framework or implementation plan for the new criminal justice systems. The Ministry of Justice's proposals for the state budget for the current year set out the challenges facing the police in relation to the ICT situation in the country. Significant funding has been allocated in 2010 and 2011 to work on new ICT infrastructure, improved information security and a pre-project for new criminal systems. Means are also available to ensure the operation of current ICT systems. It is challenging and resource-intensive to plan and implement an ICT project as large as the renewal of police criminal systems. In order to avoid misinvestment and to ensure control of the cost framework of the project, careful planning and quality assurance are important. The Directorate of Police will therefore carry out this year a draft committee study for the project which will be subject to external quality assurance. In the light of this work, I will provide the Stortinget with a detailed explanation of the ICT situation in the police in the forthcoming budget proposal. I agree that the Directorate of Police, like other state activities, should have an investment plan showing the need for initial investment and reinvestment in key areas, including ICT. However, the preparation of such an investment plan does not imply that any investment need is covered by specific appropriations. The starting point shall be that both the police districts and the Police Directorate budget and plan their activities so that the needs can be addressed within the framework in force at all times. However, it will be such that any investment needs requiring special appropriations will be presented to the Magnitude in the ordinary course of time. The government will come back to this if necessary. The government's 2011 draft budget is firm. I am very concerned about both ICT investment, the staffing of the floor and the work of securing work for newly educated students. The 2011 State budget therefore announced that increased provision for ICT could primarily have implications for other police projects. The Government will follow up the target of two police officers per thousand by 2020, as signalled in the Police Directorate Report ‘Police against 2020’. The Government also follows in the 2011 budget the recommendations set out in the report, including: by continuing the admission to the Police College with 720 students. The allocation has also been strengthened to facilitate the provision of jobs to new trainees in the building.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-03-15.ana.ud2502e2777	The right considers that the general rule in Norwegian working life should be so that you can dress as you wish and that it should be allowed to mark your religious identity. It has been decided that the police should not use religious symbols and headgear. The same should apply to judges. Judges shall exercise their judgments in a manner that provides confidence and respect. It follows from the ethical principles of judicial behaviour that a judge shall act in such a way that no question may be asked about the neutrality of the judge. The instruction authority of Stortinget vis-à-vis the Court of Justice should be regarded as a safety valve which should only be used in exceptional cases. So acute and serious, we don't think this case is. ParlaMint-NO_2011-03-15.ana.ud2502e2874	It is of great importance that the courts and their judges always have high confidence in society. However, I would advise against the use of the instructional authority The King of State Council has been provided with the third paragraph of Section 33 of the Court Act, and will explain this in more detail. The Courts of Justice are independent and the Administrative Board of the Court of Justice is an independent body governed by its own Governing Board. The administration of the Court of Justice cannot be instructed by the Ministry of Justice. This division of responsibilities is laid down by Stortinget in the Law of the Court of Justice. Guidelines for the activities of the Court of Justice are normally provided by the Grand Duchy through budgetary proposals. In addition, the King of the Council of State may, as referred to above, decide on the activities in accordance with the provisions of the third paragraph of Section 33 of the Law of the Court of Justice, provided that the Administrative Board of the Court of Justice is given the opportunity to give its opinion in advance before the decision is taken. It is clear from the law-makers that this means of instruction must be seen as a safety valve to be used only in exceptional cases. It shall not be applicable as a general and general government authority to review the decisions of the Court of Justice. Until now, the power to take decisions on the activities of the courts has not been exercised. It would not be advisable to apply the authorising authority in this case. The Court of Justice has decided to confer on the independent body the power of the administration of the Court of Justice to lay down a regulation on appeals in the courts. In that case, the King of the Council of State should not instruct the Administrative Board of the Court of Justice on how to use their regulatory competence, in particular when the Administrative Board of the Court of Justice itself has given a thorough consideration and 14 June 2010 concluded that it is not amending the Code of Conduct of the Court of Justice. If a wish is to introduce a ban on the use of religious and political symbols in justice, this should be done through a legislative amendment. Then the Storting will also have the opportunity to address the fundamental questions that such a proposal could raise, through a broad and thorough discussion. As this assembly has already pointed out, the Labour Party's Committee on Integration has proposed a ban on the use of religious and political symbols in court, which proposes that the ban apply to professional judges, but not to felons. This proposal is to be examined at the National Labour Party meeting in April. The government will decide whether it will promote a bill prohibiting political and religious symbols in court once the government parties have settled the issue.
