ParlaMint-NO_2011-03-15.ana.ud2502e3096	The topic addressed by the interpellant illustrates the potential conflict between the principle of disclosure and the legal protection of individuals against undesirable publicity. Dissemination of information by the press about what is happening in the courtroom may represent the greatest burden of being involved in criminal proceedings. In the case of insults, the media review may be seen as a strengthening and repetition of the acts involved in the criminal proceedings. The media therefore have a great responsibility for how they choose to describe this type of case. At the same time, it will not always be in the interests of the victims that the media fail to refer from the cases either. In some cases it may be that the victims themselves want cases brought to light, and in this sense media press coverage can be a good tool. I want to make it clear that I can't comment on the Alvdal case in concrete terms. It is a fundamental principle that the courts are independent in their judicial function, and as Minister of Justice, it is wrong to comment on a particular case that is pending before the courts. It is important for me to ensure that the independence of the courts is respected. The principle of public access is regarded as an important guarantee for the safeguarding of justice and is also enshrined in the European Convention on Human Rights and in the UN Convention on Civil and Political Rights. Public access to justice provides an opportunity to control and criticise the actors. First and foremost, it is considered a guarantee against infringement by the courts or prosecutors. It can also be seen as a guarantee against discrimination in favour of each intended person. Although this does not have such a real meaning for us in today's conditions, publicity in any case helps to increase confidence that everything goes right and proper. Public disclosure is also a prerequisite for the press to be able to communicate what is happening in Norwegian courts to most people. The fact that the press mentions the matter and, where appropriate, the angle given to the matter can undoubtedly be seen as a burden for the parties involved – with a view, insult and relatives. The Court Act contains rules that balance transparency with other overriding interests. The Court of First Instance may, inter alia: decide that a court meeting shall be held in whole or in part for closed doors if, for example: the interests of privacy or honesty so warrant. Furthermore, in certain cases, the Court of First Instance may, in whole or in part, prohibit the public reproduction of the negotiations if the conditions for holding the proceedings for closed doors otherwise exist. For the sake of order, I also mention that the Law of the Court of Justice allows the court to prohibit the public reproduction of all or part of the court decision if privacy considerations or the ignominious behaviour require it. It is the right to decide on the application of these rules in each case. The interests of offended and relatives will be taken care of by the aid lawyer, but the prosecution authority can also take care of the interests through, for example: to ask for closed doors. In my view, it is important to ensure that particular attention is paid to children who have been subjected to abuse. In this respect, I would like to mention that the government has invested heavily in the establishment of a nationwide system of children's housing, which is now located in Bergen, Tromsø, Kristiansand, Trondheim, Hamar, Oslo and Stavanger. Children’s homes ensure that justice can meet their children in a more gentle manner, while helping to ensure a good and complete provision of assistance and treatment in one place. As I was at the outset, the press has an independent responsibility for what they choose to publish. It has not been given that the press may or shall publish all that it is aware of, even if a court meeting or a court decision is public. The Norwegian Press Association has adopted the Varsom poster, which contains press ethics rules to be followed by both editors and the individual journalist. Furthermore, I would also like to point out that the Editorial plaque, adopted by the National Association of Media Companies and Norwegian Editorial Association, shows that the editor in charge has personal responsibility for the content of the media. The editor’s poster also shows that the editor is to ensure freedom of expression and, to the best of his ability, to work for what serves society in his/her opinion. This is an obligation. On 17 July 2009, the government appointed the so-called Media Responsibility Committee. The selection includes, among others: mandated to assess the criminal liability of the editor and the need for specific legal rules to ensure the privacy of individuals in relation to the media. The committee will give its opinion to the Ministry of Culture next month, i.e. in April. The Alvdal case illustrates the timeliness of these aspects of the mandate of the sample, and the investigation will therefore be very useful for further work on this particular issue.

It's a good principle, even in a matter where, according to the media, there were obvious perpetrators and the guilt question was settled at an early stage. In this case, which was repeatedly mentioned in the media, over several months in advance of the proceedings, it may have provided for some influence and prejudice. I'm not saying that this was an intentional influence, but with such grotesque descriptions that appeared in the press, it wouldn't be unnatural if people both in court and out in the population were slightly affected. On the other hand, the court opens up as much openness as possible. This gives the press freer ropes, but they still have both the care-pad and the Editor's poster as a guide. We are hanging over both openness and freedom of expression as two pillars of democracy. These are important foundations that must not be laid too far, nor in a courtroom. In conclusion, I would like to point out that transparency about infringement cases requires a conscious and responsible press. I am therefore pleased that today we are taking up the debate on where the limits should go for how detailed the press should be in its coverage of such cases. The press coverage of these kinds of cases must be based on the fact that insulting should not have to experience the coverage of the press as enough interference.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-03-15.ana.ud2502e3236	It's late in the evening, but it's nice to see the Council of State holding out. I have invited to a debate on paperless, and in particular the group of paperless people who are not forced to return. Our task as politicians is to make laws and govern the country after them, but we must at all times dryly discuss whether the laws we have serve society or not. Several speakers for the government have said several times that the law should be the same for everyone on the issue that we are debating today. I think we all agree on that. But we politicians can't hide us either behind the law or the foreign administration, for that matter, in relation to the group of people we're talking about here. I have also registered that representatives of the government do not like the term ‘paperless’. They think it's inaccurate. Rather, the government likes to use the term ‘illegal’ or ‘illegal’ immigrants. In this debate, I would like to highlight part of this group, namely those who have been in Norway for very many years, and especially those who have nowhere else to go – yes, those who cannot even force the police to return. So we're talking about a number of people between 1,500 and 3,000 who, for different reasons, can't be forced to return. What the law already says today is that the administration can provide humanitarian accommodation ‘with special attention’. Such consideration may be linked to Norway, but practice has nevertheless become that what is considered to be illegal residence in the country is not assessed. In order to change this, it is necessary to amend the provisions of the Foreign Affairs Act, so that all residence in the country is also relevant. One condition should then be that the person is not convicted of criminal acts. In addition, the Regulation should specify that immigration-regulating considerations should be given less weight in matters relating to children or in matters relating to them have come to Norway as a child. Among those who are in the group that we're talking about now, there are also families of children. In Norway today, in fact, we punish children for choices made by their parents. The UDI promoted a concrete proposal for legalisation drawn up in consultation with the Norwegian Centre for Human Rights at the University of Oslo in April 2003. UDI’s proposal was that ‘residence may be granted five years after final rejection if there is no doubt as to identity and eight years in case of doubt’. I would just like to take the opportunity to quote from the UDI report on long-term reception of April 2003: ‘At some point, it may be asked whether the authorities are given a responsibility to cut through and set a line. Whatever the original reason, the timing itself will raise questions about the relationship of authorities with human rights. These must be understood in such a way that, after a certain period of time, there is a need for a reassessment in which more emphasis is placed on new elements such as: fitness, rooting and adaptation to a local environment, established close relationships, training and education, local environment support, etc.’.» Do you think it's time to brush the dust of this proposal here again? In its fourth report on Norway, published in February 2009, the Council of Europe Commission on Racism and Intolerance, ECRI, strongly recommended that the Norwegian authorities should facilitate the granting of a residence permit for non-citizens who, for practical reasons, cannot return to their country of origin. Such a strong recommendation would suggest that the Norwegian authorities would change the law, or in some way clear up the situation. But the answer is provisional: return, return, return. There are at least two groups of undetectable immigrants. In one group, those that are really untraceable are, for example: Palestinians from Gaza, where the border is closed, and Somalia. The second group is those who are from countries without return agreements, regimes such as Iran, Ethiopia and Eritrea. Here we know that the government is working to establish a return agreement. But the question we have to ask ourselves is, because these return agreements are not in place, and you've been working on it for a long time, and you don't know how long it will take: how long are these people going to have to put their lives on hold? That should also be addressed in this debate. Should it not be that asylum seekers who have not been able to return to Norway five years after the final refusal may be allowed to regularise their stay in Norway? Wouldn't it have been possible with criteria such as that the identity is known and that the person concerned is not convicted of criminal acts? In addition, with a five-year limit, you will normally have been here for seven years or more, including the length of the procedure. The scheme would involve a long and difficult waiting time that would not make Norway more attractive to asylum seekers who would expect to abuse the scheme. The requirements for cooperation around identity and flawless conduct will prevent possible crime. The Christian People's Party believes that there should be room for a solution here. I do not believe that resolving the situation that this group is in will lead to Norway being flooded with a new asylum stream. There are other countries in Europe that have made such a regularisation. Sweden carried out extensive regularisation in 2006, when 17 000 rejected asylum seekers were allowed to stay after a new examination of the application. Belgium carried out its most recent regularisation in 2009, when around 25 000 people stayed. At the latest regularisation in the Netherlands, in 2007, about 30 000 stay. It is also worth noting that other countries besides the regularisations also have basic provisions that differ significantly from Norwegian approaches. For example, in France, 18-year-olds in France are normally entitled to French citizenship if they have attended school in France for at least three years. The Maria Amelie case put the situation of the paperless on the agenda. Organisations in this field have estimated that there are from 1500 to 3000 people who have been in Norway without a legal stay for more than five years. We are talking about a group of people who, for various reasons, cannot be forced to return. According to the 'no human beings are illegal' campaign, these persons cannot be forced to return with the Foreign Office of the Police. Thus, people are in a limbo situation where their subjective perception of their situation in their home country means that they do not dare to return voluntarily, while Norwegian authorities cannot force them to return to their home country. Most other European countries have had different legal systems for long-term residents without legal residence. Norway has not had any. That's why I ask the Council of State in this interview: how do the Council of State view the situation of the paperless, and how do the Council of State view the possibility of resolving this situation for the paperless? I know that this is not a simple debate, and there are rarely simple answers. But at least my call is that we look at what was UDI's proposal in 2003, which is about regularising people who had been in Norway for a long time. We're also talking about families with children here. So there are children who have either been taken with their parents, or who are born in Norway, who are in this limbo situation. I think we should do something about this, and I look forward to the further debate.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-03-15.ana.ud2502e3277	Everyone seeking protection in Norway is given a thorough and individual treatment of their case. Those at risk of persecution and abuse in their country of origin are protected in Norway. Last year, UDI and UNE were granted protection for over 4 700 people. The UDI and UNE also assess in each case whether there are strong human considerations or links with the State that require authorisation even if the conditions for protection are not met. This also applies where the non-resident has received a final rejection, but subsequently the UN requests that the rejection decision be converted. Last year, UDI and UNE granted humanitarian authorisations to more than 900 people. All applicants for protection in Norway shall be assessed in accordance with the same legislation. Nothing is the same, but the law is the same for everyone. If you do not fulfil the conditions of stay, you are obliged to leave the country. In order to maintain trust in the system, it is important that we have legislation that takes care of non-discrimination and does not have rules that encourage people to stay despite final rejection. For the work of the Foreign Service to function, it is necessary that non-residents applying for this purpose cooperate with the authorities. This also applies in the final phase of the application process. More people live without a legal stay in Norway, e.g. upon final rejection of the application for protection. Others travel illegally into the country without applying for any permission. Some of these stay here for many years. Before I go into the problem around this group, I would first like to make it clear that I understand the interpellant there that he means people without legal residence in Norway with ‘paperless’. This group must not be confused with persons who are missing ID papers or other documents from their home country. Many who live without a residence permit in Norway have ID documents. For those who do not, it is often possible to obtain it from Norway via the home country’s embassy or consulate. Persons without legal residence in Norway are a composite group. In some, very few, cases, the stranger cannot return home even if he wishes. We have seen the need to grant a residence permit in Norway in such a situation, provided that practical obstacles do not exist to the foreigner himself. We therefore have a provision in the Foreign Regulation which ensures humanitarian residence in these cases. However, the conditions here are that the foreigner has contributed to the return and that there is no doubt about the identity of the applicant. However, the majority of those in Norway without a permit are not in such a situation. The interpellant refers to persons who cannot be forced to return by the Foreign Office of the Police. The reasons why some cannot be forced to return include: that it is often not possible to issue travel documents without the individual contributing, or that the authorities of the applicant’s home country only receive returnees on a voluntary basis. As a rule, there is nothing to prevent the foreigner from handling the necessary papers and returning them on his own. Where, after a thorough and legal examination of the case, the foreigner has found that there is no need for protection, the applicant may not choose whether to comply with the decision or not. Let me remind you that the government is constantly working to provide the best possible facilities for voluntary returns, which is the best for all parties. With IM as an operator, we offer a well-organised return facility that is free of charge for the applicants. The interpellant calls for a solution for persons residing here without authorisation. I believe the solution is assisted return to the home country with reintegration support. There is no doubt that it is a great burden to be in a situation where life is put on hold, and especially for children, this is an unsustainable existence. Children are not guilty of choosing their parents, but they often suffer harm. Although today’s legislation and practices pay particular attention to children’s relationship with Norway, the government wants to look further at the situation of children who have lived illegally for a long time in Norway, in the wake of the child-breeding announcement. It must be stressed, however, that it is not desirable that, in such a situation, and not least children, it should be possible for them to be an instrument for obtaining a residence permit for which the conditions are not initially fulfilled. Such a provision could also attract more asylum seekers who do not have a need for protection and also undermine the motivation for voluntary return. We see that more and more people choose to travel on a voluntary basis. In 2010, over 1,400 people voluntarily returned to their country of origin with the support of the Norwegian authorities, which is an increase of 30 pst. This is very positive. In addition, 4,600 persons were transported by coercion. Interpellant states that other European countries have had different legalisation arrangements for this group and that Norway has had none. Persons who have received a final rejection have unlimited access to request UNE to reverse the rejection decision. Here, the connection to the kingdom, especially in matters relating to children, will be a central moment. However, an overall assessment must be carried out, where other considerations may also be relevant and decisive. I would also like to stress that this government does not want long-term illegal residence to be able to justify a permit alone. I would also like to stress that the rules and measures on the regularisation of illegal residence in other countries are not necessarily more liberal than Norwegian rules. For example, several southern European countries have implemented large and comprehensive regularisations in order to obtain a labour market with a large irregular workforce into order. We do not have such a situation in Norway. In these cases, there are mainly reasons other than purely humanitarian ones that have led to legalisation. Other countries, such as the Netherlands, France and Sweden, have implemented similar schemes in very special situations, such as: in the case of conversions of an overloaded immigration management. Nor is this the case in Norway. On the contrary, we see a decrease in the number of people seeking protection here. In 2009, a total of 17,226 people applied, while last year only 10,064 asylum seekers were registered in Norway. Finally, I would like to stress that people who stay in Norway for a long time without a residence permit represent a complex issue, which requires an overall and principleal approach and not an easy-to-use solution.

Of those coming to Norway who are here to contribute to society. All the focus in all debates is on a small, small proportion of the total number. Now we're talking about the so-called paperless, which is a concept I don't have any sense of. But the remaining 80-90 pst. which is to be integrated into the Norwegian society, which is to participate in Norwegian working life, to participate in Norwegian social life, is sacrificed to a small extent. We had an interpellation a few weeks ago at Per-Kristian Foss about Poles who become unemployed in Norway and who do not get access to Norwegian training if they are not so lucky to live in Oslo and Akershus, because there is some special course. This type of problem is very often put in the background of a debate on a small group that is illegal here, who does not want to return to their home country voluntarily, and who does not cooperate, because if they cooperate, there are many opportunities, such as the Council of State and others had in mind. It's financial assistance, there's a lot of other possibilities like training, etc. I hope we can have a debate in the future that goes on: Rules of Procedure: Reduce the number of requests for conversion. Get a faster flow. Reduce the length of the procedure in general. Take better care of those who come to Norway who are entitled to stay. Give them good offers, Norwegian training, give them work, etc. It's the most important thing, seems right.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-03-15.ana.ud2502e3394	I usually do not participate in debates on immigration and asylum policy in the main hall. But now that SV has tried an alternate strategy lately, I have decided to take the floor. I must admit that I have followed many of these debates with great interest. Often, they have appeared, from a representative who usually deals with other kinds of political issues, as a quantitative competition: we have thrown out so many and so many. We've got the applicants down. When we ruled, come and so many fewer hit here, etc. Rarely is it that one discusses the fates within this field. That is understandable. The biggest job is to adopt the lines and the rules. In debates on individuals in the Chamber, sentimentality could quickly be dropped. It's both a little constructive, and it could be speculative. Nevertheless, there is reason to remember that we define and follow principles for a reason. Rules do not exist by virtue of themselves, they do not exist in a manless vacuum. The wording of this debate has also puzzled me. When words find the way to the asylum debate, they mutate. It is no longer called the division of responsibilities. It's called burden-sharing. People in vulnerable situations, such as long-term paperless and irreversible, are reduced to a statistical burden. The presence of these people is a burden. «Flight current» means a concept which is rhetorically designed to produce the images of uncontrolled hordes that we find difficult to handle. The weight is no longer put on the flight itself or what is the reason people choose to escape, but that they together form a current. ‘Beautiful hunter’ — a term that is usually raised, especially on the right, as an honorary word, to a small extent. Seeking a better life for themselves and theirs becomes a disqualifying trait, and something we don't want here in the country. The masters in this exercise are, as we have heard today, the Progress Party. They leave the asylum debate with prejudices and fear of wiping out the human face behind the numbers. The leader of the party, the representative Jensen, has previously complained about asylum seekers who drugged and raped girls in Oslo. To believe that this use of language does not affect the climate of debate, which in turn affects the policy pursued, is the real naivety of the Norwegian asylum debate. Sometimes the prejudices are broken. The obvious confusion of the party in meeting Maria Amelie is very illustrative, for the terrain passed no longer with the completed map. The sympathy of the opinion reversed, and the government agreed on new arrangements to ensure the undocumented possibility of returning to Norway to work. I'm very happy about that. When the rules are to exist only by virtue of itself, and when the rules suddenly cannot be changed because it would no longer be the same for the law, we have forgotten what the rules are for in the first place. There is no lack of examples of us making exceptions to our rules for different reasons — not to be kind, but because it makes sense, decent and meaningful. Regulations such as: is not, as the Minister of Justice rightly points out, about being the kindest or the most liberal. I mean it's about two things, and it's propriety and control. It is unsustainable that thousands of people live in Norway for years without rights, without work, without any other means of coping than crime or undeclared work. So you could say that they weren't supposed to be here in the first place, that they had to go home, and that the rules had to be followed. But it won't last. It doesn't work like that in any other field of society. For example, we throw. not all drug addicts in prison, even if what they do is illegal. We recognize that these are people who need help and other solutions. This is what the debate on regularisation is really about. It is this that makes countries that are otherwise less liberal than Norway regulate hundreds of thousands of people, because it is recognised that none of us is served by the fact that thousands of people are without health services, without education, without vaccines and without work — with the consequences that it has for black work, crime, poverty and health. It is these people who bear the burden themselves, and many of them cannot be sent out of the country. There is no evidence that regulation schemes lead to an increase in the number of people coming to the country or that more people are trying to abuse the system. This is about taking the consequence that people are in Norway for 5, 10, 15 and up to 17 years without any rights. No one deserves it. I am pleased that this debate is being taken, much thanks to the campaign 'No human beings are illegal'. It's new to me that the church is a left-wing radical secondary organization, but that's the concept of the Progress Party. In front of us, we have a glittering opportunity to make sure that the map is more consistent with the terrain. Because, as John Steinbeck wrote, life couldn't change the sun or win the desert, so it changed itself. As surely as the sun shines and the desert dries, it is that people will continue to flee, continue to move, continue to seek new lives, and that our rules will never take account of all eventualities. What this debate is about is to take the consequences of it.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-03-15.ana.ud2502e3463	I would like to thank the interpellant for bringing this debate into the main hall. There's been a lot of pathos in the posts here, and I'm going to need to go back to the facts. The central statistical agency suggests that 18 000 people are residing illegally in Norway. It is assumed that more than two thirds of these are asylum seekers who have been denied their asylum applications. The Interpol text states that 1500 to 3000 people have both been here illegally for more than five years and cannot be returned by the Police Foreign Unit. The representative, Bekkewold, pointed out in his statement that these are persons from countries with which Norway does not have return agreements. There is reason to question both the number and the concept of ‘paperless’, but that is not what is at the heart of today’s debate. What's important is the mention of this as a situation with only one solution, an amnesty. There is another and better solution: voluntary return. There's nothing to prevent those people that this is about, from going home voluntarily. On the contrary, the government wants those who are rejected to get a good start when they are back in their home country. Norway therefore has good arrangements for those who choose to travel on a voluntary basis. The Labour Party is committed to ensuring that all those at risk of persecution, torture or inhumane treatment on return to their country of origin have the right – and have – to stay in Norway. This is done after a thorough assessment of each individual asylum application. At the same time, we must ensure that the institution of asylum has legitimacy and support. This means that those who are refused must go home. There has so far been a broad consensus on the return policy. A unanimous increase was in favour of the EU Return Directive in December. This Decision includes, inter alia: that more people with illegal residence will be expelled with an entry ban. A legalisation system, an amnesty, will in practice reward those who have not acted upon decisions taken by the Norwegian authorities. Such an arrangement will be perceived as an invitation to go underground and endure until the next time there is a similar arrangement. The Workers' Party does not want it that way. And so today's opposition parties, the Christian People's Party included, would not have it when they were in government. Minister of State Erna Solberg, Bondevik, stressed in 2004 that ‘we cannot have a system that accepts people ignoring legal decisions’. That is an assessment I fully agree with. It may not be that the individual asylum seeker chooses whether to stay in the country, even if he or she should comply with Norwegian law or not. Although a general legalisation system is not the way to go, there is reason to look at the situation of children. I am pleased that the government has notified a matter to the Stortinget about how the asylum applications for children are being treated on the run and followed up. In addition, there is reason to consider how the Norwegian society deals with people who live here illegally, no matter what reason. In particular, it is important that everyone is required to provide a health service. This is what the government is working on. That's good. Having said that, the most important thing the government is doing to solve the challenge of people who are staying illegally is to ensure a rapid procedure and a rapid return for those who are being rejected. It will prevent persons without a right to protection from entering and remaining illegal in Norway. It's important to them it touches directly, they get started faster with the rest of their lives. But it is also important for the establishment and trust of the asylum institution. Norwegian law and decisions taken by Norwegian authorities must be followed by everyone in Norway — by everyone.

It's the proposals No 1-3, from Morten Ørsal Johansen on behalf of the Party of Progress and the Right Proposal No. 1 sounds: ‘The government is asked to submit a plan for ICT investment in the police force to the Stortinget.’ Proposal No. 2 sounds: ‘The government asks the Government to assume that the planned ICT investments are not carried out at the expense of the police’s ordinary operating budget, including the recruitment of newly educated persons and the target of 2.0 police per 1 000 inhabitants.’ Proposal No. 3 sounds: ‘The government is asked to come back with a plan for ICT investment in the police, as well as the costs of this project, in the context of the revised national budget. The plan must assume that ICT investment does not go beyond staffing in the establishment.» The Christian People's Party has announced their support for the proposals. Alternatively, there is a vote between the proposals and the Committee's position.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-03-15.ana.ud2502e4028	Two proposals have been put forward during the debate. It's proposal No 1, by Hans Frode Kielland Asmyhr on behalf of the Progress Party and Right-wing Proposal No. 2, from Kjell Ingolf Ropstad on behalf of the Christian People's Party Proposal No. 1 is included in the setting, while proposal No. 2 is distributed in the seats of the representatives in the hall. As regards proposal No: 2, it has been surrendered after the period laid down in the first paragraph of Section 30 of the Storting Rules of Procedure. If there are no objections to it, the President will nevertheless allow the proposal to be put to the vote. – It shall be deemed to have been adopted.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-03-15.ana.ud2502e4044	First of all, there is a vote on proposal No. 1 from the Advancement Party and the Right. The proposal reads as follows: ‘The government is asking the government to submit a bill to the Stortinget that a provision prohibiting the use of religious and political symbols in court is included in the Law of the Court of Justice.’
ParlaMint-NO_2011-03-15.ana.ud2502e4059	There is a vote on proposal No. 2, from the Christian People's Party. The proposal reads as follows: ‘The Government asks the Government to submit a legislative proposal to ensure that professionals have neutral clothing in the courts.’ The Progress Party and the Right have notified that they wish to support this proposal.
